\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{00000000000000}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{00000000000000}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{00000000000000}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{00000000000000}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{00000000000000}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{00000000000000}

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{00000000000000}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{00000000000000}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{00000000000000}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{00000000000000}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{00000000000000}\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{00000000000000}

\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{00000000000000}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{00000000000000}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{00000000000000}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{00000000000000}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{00000000000000}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{00000000000000}

\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{00000000000000}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phantom{00000000000000}\phantom{00000000000000}\phantom{00000000000000}\phantom{00000000000000}\phantom{00000000000000}

This is the first 243 digits of the 7-Singer sequence,
\textbf{S}(7)=(s^{(7)}_{i})_{i\geq 0}=\tau_{7}(\varphi_{7}^{\infty}(1))
.

3.3.3.
Symmetries of the Pseudo-
p
-Singer Sequence

The goal is to establish an analogue of Lemma
3.4
for the
p
-Singer sequence. Before this can be achieved, a similar lemma must be stated for the
pseudo
-
p
-Singer sequence.

Lemma 3.9
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and any
0\leq i\leq p_{2}^{(k)}
, one has that
s_{p,i}=s_{p,p^{(k)}_{2}-i}
.

Proof.

This is done by induction. In the base case where
k=1
, one has
0\leq i\leq p_{2}
, and therefore,

s_{p,i}={p_{2}\choose i}\mod p={p_{2}\choose p_{2}-i}\mod p=s_{p,p_{2}-i},

as required.

For the inductive step, assume that the values of
s_{p,i}
for
0\leq i\leq p^{(k)}_{2}
satisfy
s_{p,i}=s_{p,p^{(k)}_{2}-i}
. For
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
, define the finite word
B(n,m)
of length
m+1\in\mathbb{N}
, where for
0\leq i\leq m
,

B(n,m)_{i}=n\cdot{m\choose i}\mod p,

and define
\textbf{0}_{n}=0\dots 0
as the finite word of length
n
comprised only of zeroes. Note that, for
n\in\Gamma_{p}
, one has that
\varphi_{p}(n)=B\left(n,p_{2}\right)*\textbf{0}_{p_{2}}
, where
*
is from Definition
2.1
. Hence, applying
\varphi_{p}
to the word
s^{(p)}_{0}*s^{(p)}_{1}*\dots*s^{(p)}_{p^{(k)}_{2}}
yields

B\left(s^{(p)}_{0},p_{2}\right)*\textbf{0}_{p_{2}}*B\left(s^{(p)}_{1},p_{2}\right)*\textbf{0}_{p_{2}}*\dots*B\left(s^{(p)}_{p^{(k)}_{2}}*p_{2}\right)*\textbf{0}_{p_{2}}.

This has length
\frac{p^{k+1}+p}{2}
. Removing the final
p_{2}
entries leaves a word of length
\frac{p^{k+1}+1}{2}
, which is symmetric by the inductive hypothesis and the symmetry of the binomial coefficients.
∎

3.3.4.
The Symmetries of the

p
-Singer Sequence

The counterpart to Lemma
3.4
is now established.

Lemma 3.10
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the
p
-Singer sequence
\textbf{S}(p)=\left(s_{i}^{(p)}\right)_{i\geq 0}
satisfies
s^{(p)}_{i}=s^{(p)}_{p^{k}+1-i}
for all
0\leq i\leq p^{k}+1
.

Proof of Lemma
3.10
.

The claim is proved by induction on
k\in\mathbb{N}
. The base case where
k=1
is immediate from the symmetry of the binomial coefficients.

A similar argument to that of Lemma
3.9
is now used to show that
s^{(p)}_{i}=s^{(p)}_{p^{k}+1-i}
for all
i
. For
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
, define the finite word

S(n,m)=n\cdot{m\choose 0}*0*n\cdot{m\choose 1}*0*n\cdot{m\choose 2}*0*\dots*0*n\cdot{m\choose m}.

Above, each
n\cdot{m\choose i}
term is taken modulo
p
. Note that
S(n,m)
has length
2m+1
and is symmetric. For
n\in\Gamma_{p}
, one has that
\tau_{p}(n)=S(n,\frac{p+1}{2})*\textbf{0}_{p-2}
, which has length
2p
. Hence, the symmetric (by Lemma
3.9
) finite word
s_{p,0}*s_{p,1}*\dots*s_{p,p^{(k)}_{2}}
is mapped by
\tau_{p}
to

S\left(s_{p,0},\frac{p+1}{2}\right),\textbf{0}_{p-2},S\left(s_{p,1},\frac{p+1}{2}\right),\textbf{0}_{p-2},\dots,S\left(s_{p,\frac{p^{k}-1}{2}},\frac{p+1}{2}\right),\textbf{0}_{p-2}.

This word has length
p^{k+1}+p
. Hence, by Lemma
3.9
, removing the final
\textbf{0}_{p-2}
returns a symmetric sequence of length
p^{k+1}+2
given by
\left(s^{(p)}_{i}\right)
for
0\leq i\leq p^{k+1}+1
.
∎

3.4.
The Relationship Between the
p
-Cantor and
p
-Singer Sequences

The similarities in the construction of the
p
-Cantor and
p
-Singer sequences suggests a possible relationship between the two. In this subsection, this is made explicit through the language of
formal Laurent series
.

To this end, define the field of formal Laurent series over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
as

\mathbb{F}_{p}((t^{-1}))=\left\{\sum_{i=-h}^{\infty}a_{i}t^{-i}:h\in\mathbb{Z},a_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}\right\}.

Next, let

\Theta_{p}(t)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}c_{i}^{(p)}t^{-i}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{ and }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \Xi_{p}(t)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}s_{i}^{(p)}t^{-i}.

This subsection establishes the following lemma:

Lemma 3.11
.

For any odd prime
p
,

(3.8)

\Theta_{p}(t)\cdot\Xi_{p}(t)=1.

That is, the
p
-Cantor and
p
-Singer sequences are ‘inverse’ to one another from the point of view of Laurent series.

Lemma
3.11
is an immediate corollary of Lemmata (
3.12
) and (
3.13
).

3.4.1.
The Laurent Series

\Theta_{p}(t)

To establish Lemma
3.11
, a closed form for
\Theta_{p}(t)
is computed.

Lemma 3.12
.

For any odd prime
p
, one has
\Theta_{p}(t)=(1+t^{-2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}((t^{-1}))
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that

(3.9)

\Theta_{p}(t)=\Theta_{p}(t)^{p}\sum_{i=0}^{p_{2}}{p_{2}\choose i}t^{-2i}.

Indeed, rearranging this equation gives

(3.10)

\Theta(t)^{p-1}=\left(\sum_{i=0}^{p_{2}}{p_{2}\choose i}t^{-2i}\right)^{-1}.

The denominator of the right hand side of equation (
3.10
) is the binomial expansion of

(1+t^{-2})^{p_{2}}.

Thus,

\Theta_{p}(t)=(1+t^{-2})^{-\frac{1}{2}},

as required. All that remains is to prove (
3.9
). To achieve this, one extends the definition of a
p
-morphism to apply to Laurent series in the following natural way:

Let
\phi:\Gamma_{p}^{\infty}\to\Gamma_{p}^{\infty}
be a
p
-morphism and let
\Theta(t)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}s_{i}t^{-i}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}((t^{-1}))
be a Laurent series whose coefficients are given by a sequence
(s_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
. To reduce notation,
x\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
is identified with
x\in\Gamma_{p}
. Then, define

(3.11)

\phi(\Theta(t))=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}t^{-pi}\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}\phi(s_{i})_{j}t^{-j}.

Let
\phi_{p}
be as in Definition
3.2
. As
\mathbf{C}^{(p)}=\phi_{p}\left(\mathbf{C}^{(p)}\right)
by definition, one has that
\Theta_{p}(t)=\phi_{p}(\Theta_{p}(t))
and hence,

\displaystyle\Theta_{p}(t):=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}c^{(p)}_{j}t^{-j}=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum_{i=0}^{p}{p_{2}\choose\frac{i}{2}}c_{j}^{(p)}t^{-(pj+i)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum_{i=0}^{p_{2}}{p_{2}\choose i}c_{j}^{(p)}t^{-(pj+2i)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{p_{2}}{p_{2}\choose i}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}c_{j}^{(p)}t^{-(pj+2i)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{p_{2}}{p_{2}\choose i}\Theta_{p}(t^{p})t^{-2i}.

Finally, as
\Theta(t^{p})=\Theta(t)^{p}
holds for any Laurent series
\Theta(t)\in\mathbb{F}_{p}((t^{-1}))
, the proof is complete.
∎

3.4.2.
The

p
-Singer Sequence is the Inverse to the
p
-Cantor Sequence

Recall that
\left(s_{p,i}\right)_{i\geq 0}=\varphi^{\infty}_{p}(1)
is the
p
-Singer sequence before the coding
\tau_{p}
is applied.

Lemma 3.13
.

For any odd prime
p
,
\Xi_{p}(t)=\left(1+t^{-2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}((t^{-1}))
.

Proof.

Define
\overline{\Xi}(t):=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}s_{p,i}t^{-i}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}((t^{-1}))
. As
\left(s_{p,i}\right)_{i\geq 0}
is the fixed point of
\varphi_{p}
, one has that

\displaystyle\overline{\Xi}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}s_{p,i}t^{-pi}\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}{p_{2}\choose j}\cdot t^{-j}

\displaystyle=\overline{\Xi}(t^{p})\cdot(1+t^{-1})^{p_{2}}

\displaystyle=\overline{\Xi}(t)^{p}\cdot(1+t^{-1})^{p_{2}}.

Hence,

\overline{\Xi}(t)=\left(1+t^{-1}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Applying the coding
\tau_{p}
to
\overline{\Xi}(t)
(in the sense of equation (
3.11
)) gives the Laurent series

(3.12)

\displaystyle\Xi_{p}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}s_{p,i}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{p_{2}+1}{p_{2}+1\choose j}t^{-2j}\right)t^{-2pi}

\displaystyle=\overline{\Xi}(t^{2p})\cdot(1+t^{-2})^{p_{2}+1}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{(1+t^{-2})^{p+1}}{1+t^{-2p}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(3.13)

\displaystyle=\left(1+t^{-2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\left(\frac{(1+t^{-2})^{p}}{1+t^{-2p}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Since
\Phi(t^{p})=\Phi(t)^{p}
for any
\Phi(t)\in\mathbb{F}_{p}((t^{-1}))
,
\Xi_{p}(t)=\left(1+t^{-2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
as required.
∎

4.
Introduction to Number Walls

Recall the definition of a number wall (Definition
1.5
). The goal of this section is to introduce the fundamental properties of number walls that are used in this work. More specific lemmata on number walls are established in Sections
7
and
8
.

4.1.
Properties of Number Walls

Recall the Square Window Theorem (Theorem
1.8
). As zero regions must always appear in squares, every such zero region is bordered by a square of nonzero entries. This motivates the following definition.

Definition 4.1
.

The entries of a number wall surrounding a window are referred to as the
inner frame
. The entries surrounding the inner frame form the
outer frame
.

The entries of the inner frame are extremely well structured:

Theorem 4.2

(Lunnon,
[
Lun01
, Page 11]
)
.

The inner frame of a window with side length
l\geq 1
is comprised of 4 geometric sequences. These are along the top, left, right and bottom edges and they have ratios
P,Q,R
and
S
respectively with origins at the top left and bottom right. Furthermore, these ratios satisfy the relation

\frac{PS}{QR}=(-1)^{l}.

See Figure
2
for an example of a window of side length
l
. For
i\in\{0,\dots,l+1\}
, the inner and outer frames are labelled by the entries
A_{i},B_{i},C_{i},D_{i}
and
E_{i},F_{i},G_{i},H_{i}
respectively. The ratios of the geometric sequences comprising the inner frame are labelled as
P,Q,R
and
S
. This notation is used frequently throughout this work.

Figure 2.
Illustration of a window in a number wall. The window, inner frame and outer frame are in red, green and blue, respectively.

4.2.
The Frame Constraints

Calculating a number wall from its definition is a computationally exhausting task. The following theorem gives a simple and far more efficient way to calculate the
m^{\textup{th}}
row using the previous rows.

Theorem 4.3

(
Frame Constraints
, Adiceam, Nesharim and Lunnon,
[
ANL21
, Corollary 3.6]
)
.

Given a doubly infinite sequence
\mathbf{S}=(s_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
over an integral domain
\mathcal{R}
, the number wall
W_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathbf{S}):=(W_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathbf{S})[m,n])_{n,m\in\mathbb{Z}}
can be generated by a recurrence in row
m\in\mathbb{Z}
in terms of the
previous rows. To state this precisely, recall the notation of Figure
2
and let
(W_{m,n})_{m,n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be the 2-dimensional sequence satisfying the initial conditions

W_{m,n}=\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} &0&&\textup{ if }m<-1&\\
&1&&\textup{ if }m=-1;&\\
&s_{n}&&\textup{ if }m=0;&\end{aligned}\end{cases}

and the recurrence relations

(4.1)

W_{m,n}=\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} &0&&\textup{ if }W_{m,n}\textup{ is within a window;}&\\
&\frac{W_{m-1,n}^{2}-W_{m-1,n-1}W_{m-1n+1}}{W_{m-2,n}}&&\textup{ if }m>0\textup{ and }W_{m-2,n}\neq 0;&\\
&D_{k}=\frac{(-1)^{l\cdot k}B_{k}C_{k}}{A_{k}}&&\textup{ if }m>0\textup{ and }W_{m-2,n}=0=W_{m-1,n};&\\
&H_{k}=\frac{\frac{QE_{k}}{A_{k}}+(-1)^{k}\frac{PF1_{k}}{B_{k}}-(-1)^{k}\frac{SG_{k}}{C_{k}}}{R/D_{k}}&&\textup{ if }m>0\textup{ and }W_{n,m-2}=0\neq W_{n,m-1}.&\end{aligned}\end{cases}

Above, the value of
k
above is found in the natural way from the value of
m,n
and the side length
l
. Additionally, “
W_{m,n}
is within a window" means that if
W_{m,n}\neq 0
then the sequence
(W_{m,n})_{m,n\in\mathbb{Z}}
would have a non-square zero portion.

Then, for all
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
W_{m,n}=W_{\mathcal{R}}(\textbf{S})[m,n]
.

The final three equations in (
4.1
) are known as the
First
,
Second
and
Third
Frame Constraint Equations
. These allow the number wall of a sequence to be considered independently of its original definition in terms of Toeplitz matrices. Throughout this paper, the three Frame Constraints are frequently referred to as
FC1
,
FC2
and
FC3
respectively.

4.3.
Finite Number Walls

Whilst a number wall is defined for doubly infinite sequences, the definition can easily be extended to allow for finite sequences. Indeed, given a finite sequence
\mathbf{S}
over an integral domain
\mathcal{R}
, the
finite number wall

W_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathbf{S})
is a two-dimensional infinite array defined as follows:

W_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathbf{S})[m,n]=\begin{cases}\det(T_{\textbf{S}}(m,n))&\textup{ if }m\geq 0\text{ and every entry of }T_{\textbf{S}}(m,n)\text{ is defined},\\
\text{an unknown variable }&\textup{ if }m\geq 0\text{ and not all entries of }T_{\textbf{S}}(m,n)\text{ are defined},\\
1&\textup{ if }m=-1,\\
0&\textup{ if }m<-1.\end{cases}

An example of a finite number wall is illustrated below.

Figure 3.
Each dot represents an entry in a number wall. The finite sequence (light green dots) that generates the finite number wall (whole picture) is on row zero. Each dot represents an entry in the finite number wall, with the dark green dots being those that are known explicitly and the black dots being those that are still variables.

It is elementary to see that a finite sequence of length
r
defines the portion of a finite number wall in the shape of an isosceles triangle with the maximal row index being equal to
\left\lfloor\frac{r-1}{2}\right\rfloor
. Therefore, it may seem paradoxical that a
finite
number wall is actually an
infinite
two-dimensional array instead a finite triangular array. However, defining it this way is advantageous later on when one wishes to rotate, translate, reflect and composite finite number walls. In practice though, only the known entries of
W_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathbf{S})
are of interest. Therefore, the unknown entries are often ignored in figures, as below.

Figure 4.
The finite number wall of a sequence over
\mathbb{F}_{5}
. The zero entries are in red, whereas the numbers 1-4 are illustrated in progressively darker shades of gray. The top row has index
-2
.

4.4.
The Profile of a Number Wall

In all known applications of number walls, the zero entries carry the most important information. That is, it only matters whether an entry is zero or nonzero, with the precise value in the latter case being irrelevant. This motivates the following definition:

Definition 4.4
.

Let
W_{\mathcal{R}}(\textbf{S})
be the number wall of a sequence
S
over an integral domain
\mathcal{R}
. The
profile
of
W_{\mathcal{R}}(\textbf{S})
, denoted
\chi(W_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathbf{S}))
, is a two dimensional array with the same shape as
W_{\mathcal{R}}(\textbf{S})
and with the entry in row
m
and column
n
being given by

\chi(W_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathbf{S}))[m,n]=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }W(\mathbf{S})[m,n]=0\\
X&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Above,
X
should be interpreted as an arbitrarily chosen symbol.

Figure 5.
Left
: The number wall
W_{5}(\textbf{S})
for the sequence
\textbf{S}=(1,1,3,2,1,0,0,0,2,0,2,0)\in\mathbb{F}_{5}^{12}
, where the zeroes are coloured in red.
Right
: The profile of
W_{5}(\textbf{S})
.

5.
The Number Wall of
\mathbf{C}^{(p)}
as a Two-Dimensional Automatic Sequence

The goal of this section is to define the two-dimensional morphism that generates
\chi\left(W_{p}\left(\mathbf{C}^{(p)}\right)\right)
. To this end, one must first clarify precisely what part of
\chi\left(W_{p}\left(\mathbf{C}^{(p)}\right)\right)
is claimed to be automatic.

For pictures of
W_{p}\left(\textbf{C}^{(p)}\right)
itself, see Appendix
B
.

5.1.
The Right-Side of
W_{p}\left(\mathbf{C}^{(p)}\right)
.

Recall that the
p
-Cantor sequence
\textbf{C}^{(p)}
is extended to a doubly infinite sequence
\textbf{C}^{(p,L)}
by defining
c^{(p)}_{i}=0
for all
i<0
. Therefore, by the Square Window Theorem (Theorem
1.8
),
W_{p}\left(\mathbf{C}^{(p,L)}\right)[m,n]=0
for all
m\geq 0
and
n<0
. That is,
W_{p}\left(\mathbf{C}^{(p,L)}\right)
has an infinite window containing all columns of negative index. Hence, one focuses only on the two-dimensional sequence
\left(W_{p}\left(\mathbf{C}^{(p,L)}\right)[m,n]\right)_{m,n\geq 0}
.

Figure 6.
An illustration of
W_{p}(\mathbf{C}^{(p,L)})
. Here, the dots represent an arbitrary element of
\mathbb{F}_{p}
. The profile of the green portion is what is shown to be automatic. Row
-1
is coloured in blue, and
W_{p}(\mathbf{C}^{(p,L)})[0,n]
for
n<0
is in red.

As seen in Section
1.4
, the fractal generated by a number wall of a sequence
S
depends only on the profile of the number wall,
\chi(W_{\mathbb{K}}(\textbf{S}))
. Hence, the two-dimensional sequence

\chi\left(\left(W_{p}\left(\mathbf{C}^{(p,L)}\right)[m,n]\right)_{m,n\geq 0}\right)

is the focus of this section. This is shown to be the image of a
[p,p]
-morphism under a
[1,1]
-coding.

5.2.
The Morphism that Generates The Right-Side of
W_{p}\left(\mathbf{C}^{(p,L)}\right)

This subsection details the finite alphabet
\mathcal{A}
, the
[p,p]
-morphism
\Phi_{p}:\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\infty}\to\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\infty}
and the
[1,1]
-coding
\Pi:\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\infty}\to\{0,X\}_{2}^{\infty}
that generates
\left(\chi\left(W_{p}\left(\mathbf{C}^{(p,L)}\right)[m,n]\right)\right)_{m,n\geq 0}
. The alphabet
\mathcal{A}
is defined first.

5.2.1.
The Finite Alphabet

\mathcal{A}

Regardless of the choice of the prime
p
, the alphabet
\mathcal{A}
consists of
12
letters -
8
of which are labelled by the cardinal or intercardinal directions:

\mathcal{A}:=\{A,\penalty\ B,\penalty\ F,\penalty\ 0,\penalty\ E_{N},\penalty\ E_{E},\penalty\ E_{S},\penalty\ E_{W},\penalty\ C_{NE},\penalty\ C_{SE},\penalty\ C_{SW},\penalty\ C_{NW}\}.

To break the definition of
\Phi_{p}
and
\Pi
up into manageable words, the alphabet
\mathcal{A}
is split into four sub-alphabets:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{units}:=\{A,B\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{zeroes}=\{F,0\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{edges}=\{E_{N},E_{E},E_{S},E_{W}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{corners}=\{C_{NE},C_{SE},C_{SW},C_{NW}\}.

Above,
E
and
C
are intended to stand for ‘edge’ and ‘corner’, respectively. For example,
E_{N}
and
C_{SW}
should be read as ‘northern edge’ and ‘south-western corner’. These labels are explained in sections
5.2.2
,
5.2.4
and
5.2.5
.

The image of each letter under
\Phi_{p}
is both defined explicitly and illustrated. With the exception of
A,B
and
F
, the images of all the above letters under
\Phi_{p}
are very simple. For those three letters, it is recommended to look at the picture
before
reading the definition.

For the remainder of this section,
0\leq m,n\leq p-1
are natural numbers and let
\Phi_{p}(\cdot)_{m,n}
be the entry in the
m^{\textup{th}}
row and the
n^{\textup{th}}
column of the image of the input under
\Phi_{p}
.

5.2.2.
The Image of

\Phi_{p}

on

\mathcal{A}_{units}

The
[p,p]
-morphism
\Phi_{p}
acts on the letters
A
and
B
by

\displaystyle\Phi_{p}(A)_{m,n}=\begin{cases}A&\text{ if }m\equiv 0\equiv n\mod 2\\
0&\text{ if }m\equiv 0\not\equiv n\mod 2\\
F&\text{ if }m\not\equiv 0\equiv n\mod 2\\
B&\text{ if }m\equiv 1\equiv n\mod 2\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle\Phi_{p}(B)_{m,n}=\begin{cases}B&\text{ if }m\equiv 0\equiv n\mod 2\\
F&\text{ if }m\equiv 0\not\equiv n\mod 2\\
0&\text{ if }m\not\equiv 0\equiv n\mod 2\\
A&\text{ if }m\equiv 1\equiv n\mod 2\\
\end{cases}

,

that is,

\displaystyle\Phi_{p}(A):=\penalty\ \begin{matrix}A&0&A&0&A&\cdots&A&0&A\\
F&B&F&B&F&\cdots&F&B&F\\
A&0&A&0&A&\cdots&A&0&A\\
F&B&F&B&F&\cdots&F&B&F\\
A&0&A&0&A&\cdots&A&0&A\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
A&0&A&0&A&\cdots&A&0&A\\
F&B&F&B&F&\cdots&F&B&F\\
A&0&A&0&A&\cdots&A&0&A\\
\end{matrix}

\displaystyle\Phi_{p}(B):=\penalty\ \begin{matrix}B&F&B&F&B&\cdots&B&F&B\\
0&A&0&A&0&\cdots&0&A&0\\
B&F&B&F&B&\cdots&B&F&B\\
0&A&0&A&0&\cdots&0&A&0\\
B&F&B&F&B&\cdots&B&F&B\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
B&F&B&F&B&\cdots&B&F&B\\
0&A&0&A&0&\cdots&0&A&0\\
B&F&B&F&B&\cdots&B&F&B\\
\end{matrix}.

5.2.3.
The Image of

\Phi_{p}

on

\mathcal{A}_{zeroes}

Define
\Phi_{p}(F)
and
\Phi_{p}(0)
as

\displaystyle\Phi_{p}(F)_{m,n}=\begin{cases}C_{NW}&\text{ if }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ m=n=0,\\
C_{NE}&\text{ if }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ m=0\penalty\ \text{ and }\penalty\ n=p-1,\\
C_{SW}&\text{ if }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ m=p-1\penalty\ \text{ and }\penalty\ n=0,\\
C_{SE}&\text{ if }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ m=n=p-1,\\
E_{N}&\text{ if }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ m=0\penalty\ \text{ and }\penalty\ 0\neq n\neq p-1,\\
E_{E}&\text{ if }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ 0\neq m\neq p-1\penalty\ \text{ and }\penalty\ n=p-1,\\
E_{S}&\text{ if }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ m=p-1\penalty\ \text{ and }\penalty\ 0\neq n\neq p-1,\\
E_{W}&\text{ if }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ 0\neq m\neq p-1\penalty\ \text{ and }\penalty\ n=0,\\
0&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\Phi_{p}(0)_{m,n}=0\text{ for all }m,n,

that is,

\displaystyle\Phi_{p}(F):=\penalty\ \begin{matrix}C_{NW}&E_{N}&E_{N}&\cdots&E_{N}&E_{N}&C_{NE}\\
E_{W}&0&0&\cdots&0&0&E_{E}\\
E_{W}&0&0&\cdots&0&0&E_{E}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\\
E_{W}&0&0&\cdots&0&0&E_{E}\\
E_{W}&0&0&\cdots&0&0&E_{E}\\
C_{SW}&E_{S}&E_{S}&\cdots&E_{S}&E_{S}&C_{SE}\end{matrix}

\displaystyle\Phi_{p}(0):=\penalty\ \begin{matrix}0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
\end{matrix}

.

Remark 5.1
.

The letter
F
was chosen to stand for the word “Frame", as the image of
F
under
\Phi_{p}
is a
p-2\times p-2
square of zeroes
framed
by the letters labelled with the corresponding cardinal or intercardinal directions. Similarly, the letters in
\mathcal{A}_{edges}
and
\mathcal{A}_{corners}
make up the edges and corners of
\Phi_{p}(F)
.

5.2.4.
The Image of

\Phi_{p}
on

\mathcal{A}_{edges}

The
[p,p]
-morphism
\Phi_{p}
acts on the elements of
\mathcal{A}_{edges}
as,

\displaystyle\Phi_{p}(E_{N})_{m,n}=\begin{cases}E_{N}&\text{ if }m=0\\
0&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

\displaystyle\Phi_{p}(E_{E})_{m,n}=\begin{cases}E_{E}&\text{ if }n=p-1\\
0&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

\displaystyle\Phi_{p}(E_{S})_{m,n}=\begin{cases}E_{S}&\text{ if }m=p-1\\
0&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

\displaystyle\Phi_{p}(E_{W})_{m,n}=\begin{cases}E_{W}&\text{ if }n=0\\
0&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

,

that is,

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}&E_{N}&E_{N}&E_{N}&\cdots&E_{N}&E_{N}&E_{N}\\
&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
\Phi_{p}(E_{N}):=&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
\\
&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
\Phi_{p}(E_{S}):=&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
&E_{S}&E_{S}&E_{S}&\cdots&E_{S}&E_{S}&E_{S}\end{matrix}

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&E_{E}\\
&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&E_{E}\\
&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0_{E}\\
\Phi_{p}(E_{E}):=&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&E_{E}\\
&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&E_{E}\\
&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&E_{E}\\
\\
&E_{W}&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
&E_{W}&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
&E_{W}&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
\Phi_{p}(E_{W}):=&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
&E_{0}&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
&E_{W}&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
&E_{W}&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\end{matrix}

5.2.5.
The Image of

\Phi_{p}

on

\mathcal{A}_{corners}

The
[p,p]
-morphism
\Phi_{p}
acts on the elements of
\mathcal{A}_{corners}
as

\displaystyle\Phi_{p}(C_{NE})_{m,n}=\begin{cases}C_{NE}&\text{ if }m=0\penalty\ \text{ and }\penalty\ n=p-1\\
E_{N}&\text{ if }m=0\penalty\ \text{ and }\penalty\ n\neq p-1\\
E_{E}&\text{ if }m\neq 0\penalty\ \text{ and }\penalty\ n=p-1\\
0&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

\displaystyle\Phi_{p}(C_{SE})_{m,n}=\begin{cases}C_{SE}&\text{ if }m=n=p-1\\
E_{S}&\text{ if }m=p-1\neq n\\
E_{E}&\text{ if }m\neq p-1=n\\
0&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

\displaystyle\Phi_{p}(C_{SW})_{m,n}=\begin{cases}C_{SW}&\text{ if }m=p-1\penalty\ \text{ and }\penalty\ n=0\\
E_{S}&\text{ if }m=p-1\penalty\ \text{ and }\penalty\ n\neq 0\\
E_{E}&\text{ if }m\neq p-1\penalty\ \text{ and }\penalty\ n=0\\
0&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

\displaystyle\Phi_{p}(C_{NW})_{m,n}=\begin{cases}C_{NW}&\text{ if }m=n=0\\
E_{N}&\text{ if }m=0\neq n\\
E_{W}&\text{ if }m\neq 0=n\\
0&\text{ otherwise, }\end{cases}

that is,

\displaystyle\Phi_{p}(C_{NW}):=\hskip-5.69046pt\begin{matrix}C_{NW}&E_{N}&E_{N}&\cdots&E_{N}&E_{N}&E_{N}\\
E_{W}&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
E_{W}&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
E_{W}&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
E_{W}&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
E_{W}&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\end{matrix}\Phi_{p}(C_{NE}):=\hskip-5.69046pt\begin{matrix}E_{N}&E_{N}&E_{N}&\cdots&E_{N}&E_{N}&C_{NE}\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&E_{E}\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&E_{E}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&E_{E}\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&E_{E}\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&E_{E}\end{matrix}

\displaystyle\Phi_{p}(C_{SW}):=\begin{matrix}E_{W}&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
E_{W}&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
E_{W}&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
E_{W}&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
E_{W}&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
C_{SW}&E_{S}&E_{S}&\cdots&E_{S}&E_{S}&E_{S}\end{matrix}\penalty\ \Phi_{p}(C_{SE}):=\begin{matrix}0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&E_{E}\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&E_{E}\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&E_{E}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&E_{E}\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&E_{E}\\
E_{S}&E_{S}&E_{S}&\cdots&E_{S}&E_{S}&C_{SE}\end{matrix}

5.2.6.
The

[1,1]
-coding
\Pi

on

\mathcal{A}

Let
L\in\mathcal{A}
. Then, the
[1,1]
-coding
\Pi:\mathcal{A}^{\infty}_{2}\to\{0,X\}_{2}^{\infty}
is defined by,

\displaystyle\Pi(L)_{m,n}=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }L=0\\
X&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

5.2.7.
Statement of Theorem
5.2

All the necessary material has been covered to allow for a full statement of Main Result 1.

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
,
\Phi_{p}:\mathcal{A}^{\infty}_{2}\to\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\infty}
and
\Pi:\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\infty}\to\{0,X\}_{2}^{\infty}
be as described above. Then,

\chi\left(\left(W_{p}\left(\mathbf{C}^{(p,L)}\right)[m,n]\right)_{m,n\in\mathbb{N}}\right)=\Pi(\Phi_{p}^{\infty}(A)).

The proof of Theorem
5.2
is completed in Section
10
. The main goal of Sections
7
-
9
is to provide the machinery needed to undertake this proof. In Section
6
, Theorem
1.9
is deduced from Theorem
5.2
.

6.
Proof of Theorem
1.9

Recall the fractal
\mathcal{F}\left(\textbf{C}^{(p)},\mathbb{F}_{p}\right)
, defined in equation (
1.4
). To prove Theorem
1.9
, two fractals
\mathcal{F}_{0,\infty},\mathcal{F}_{F,\infty}\subseteq[0,1]^{2}
are constructed, which satisfy the following properties:

(1)

\mathcal{F}_{0,\infty}\subseteq\mathcal{F}\left(\textbf{C}^{(p)},\mathbb{F}_{p}\right)\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{F,\infty}
and

(2)

\frac{\log\left(\frac{p^{2}+1}{2}\right)}{\log p}\leq\mathrm{dim}_{H}(\mathcal{F}_{0,\infty})
and
\mathrm{dim}_{H}(\mathcal{F}_{F,\infty})\leq\frac{\log\left(\frac{p^{2}+1}{2}\right)}{\log p}
.

Claim
2
is established first.

6.1.
The Fractal
\mathcal{F}_{0,\infty}

First, a simplified version of the morphism
\Phi_{p}
is defined. Let
\Phi_{0,p}:\{0,A\}_{2}^{\infty}\rightarrow\{0,A\}^{\infty}_{2}
be the
[p,p]
-morphism defined by

(6.1)

\displaystyle\Phi_{0,p}(\mathbf{0}_{p\times p})=\mathbf{0}_{p\times p}\,

\displaystyle\Phi_{0,p}(A)=\begin{matrix}A&0&A&\dots&A&0&A\\
0&A&0&\dots&0&A&0\\
A&0&A&\dots&A&0&A\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
A&0&A&\dots&A&0&A\\
0&A&0&\dots&0&A&0\\
A&0&A&\dots&A&0&A\end{matrix}.

To bound
\mathrm{dim}_{H}\mathcal{F}_{0,\infty}
from below,
[
Fal90
, Mass Distribution Principle 4.2]
is utilized. For
U\subseteq[0,1]^{2}
, define the
diameter
of
U
by
|U|:=\sup\{\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|_{2}:\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in U\}
.

Proposition 6.1
.

[
Fal90
, Mass Distribution Principle 4.2]

Let
X
be a set, and let
\mu
be a mass distribution on
X
. Suppose that there exist
C>0
and
\varepsilon>0
, such
\mu(U)\leq C|U|^{s}
, for any set
U
with
|U|<\varepsilon
. Then,
\mathrm{dim}_{H}X\geq s
.

To apply Proposition
6.1
, the number of non-zero entries in
\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(A)
is computed.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
m=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}m_{j}p^{j}
, and let
n=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}n_{j}p^{j}
, where
m_{j},n_{j}\in\{0,1,\dots,p-1\}
. Then,
\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(A)[m,n]\neq 0
if and only if
m_{j}\equiv n_{j}\mod 2
for every
j=0,1,\dots,k-1
.

To prove Lemma
6.2
, the following definition is made.

Definition 6.3
.

Let
n=\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor\log_{p}n\rfloor}n_{j}p^{j}
, where
n_{j}\in\{0,1,\dots,p-1\}
for every
j\in\{0,\dots\lfloor\log_{p}n\rfloor\}
. Define the
prefix
of
n
by
\operatorname{pref}(n)=\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor\log_{p}n\rfloor-1}n_{j}p^{j}
.

Proof of Lemma
6.2
.

The proof proceeds by induction. The case
k=1
follows from the definition of
\Phi_{0,p}(A)
. Assume that the claim holds for
k
. Let
m,n\leq p^{k+1}
be of the form
m=\sum_{j=0}^{k}m_{j}p^{j}
and
n=\sum_{j=0}^{k}n_{j}p^{j}
. Note that

(6.2)

\Phi_{0,p}^{k+1}(A)=\begin{matrix}\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(A)&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(0)&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(A)&\dots&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(A)&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(0)&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(A)\\
\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(0)&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(A)&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(0)&\dots&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(0)&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(A)&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(0)\\
\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(A)&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(0)&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(A)&\dots&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(A)&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(0)&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(A)\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(A)&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(0)&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(A)&\dots&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(A)&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(0)&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(A)\\
\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(0)&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(A)&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(0)&\dots&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(0)&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(A)&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(0)\\
\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(A)&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(0)&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(A)&\dots&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(A)&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(0)&\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(A)\end{matrix}.

As a consequence, if
m_{k}\neq n_{k}\mod 2
, then, by (
6.2
),
\Phi_{0,p}^{k+1}(A)[m,n]=0
. Otherwise, if
m_{k}\equiv n_{k}\mod 2
, then, by (
6.2
) and the induction hypothesis,
\Phi_{0,p}^{k+1}(A)[m,n]=\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(A)[\operatorname{pref}(m),\operatorname{pref}(n)]
. By the induction hypothesis,
\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(A)[\operatorname{pref}(m),\operatorname{pref}(n)]\neq 0
if and only if
m_{i}\equiv n_{i}\mod 2
for every
i=0,\dots,k-1
. As a consequence,
\Phi_{0,p}^{k+1}(A)[m,n]\neq 0
if and only if
m_{i}\equiv n_{i}\mod 2
for every
i=0,\dots,k
.
∎

By Lemma
6.2
, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathcal{I}_{k}:=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}m,n\in\mathbb{N}\\
\Phi_{0,p}^{k}(A)[m,n]\neq 0\end{subarray}}\left[\frac{m}{p^{k}},\frac{m+1}{p^{k}}\right)\times\left[\frac{n}{p^{k}},\frac{n+1}{p^{k}}\right)=\bigcup_{(m,n)\in\mathcal{N}_{k}}\left[\frac{m}{p^{k}},\frac{m+1}{p^{k}}\right)\times\left[\frac{n}{p^{k}},\frac{n+1}{p^{k}}\right),

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{k}

\displaystyle=\left\{(m,n)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}:m=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}m_{i}p^{i},n=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}n_{i}p^{i}:\forall i=0,\dots,k-1,m_{i}\equiv n_{i}\mod 2\right\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\{(m,n)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}:0\leq m,n\leq p^{k}-1\}.

Define

\mathcal{F}_{0,\infty}:=\bigcap_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{I}_{k}=\left\{(x,y)\in[0,1]^{2}:x=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}x_{n}p^{-n},y=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}y_{n}p^{-n},x_{n}\equiv y_{n}\mod 2,\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}.

The size of
\mathcal{N}_{k}
is now able to be computed.

Lemma 6.4
.

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\#\mathcal{N}_{k}=\left(\frac{p^{2}+1}{2}\right)^{k}
.

Proof.

Note that there are
\frac{p+1}{2}
even digits and
\frac{p-1}{2}
odd digits in the set
\{0,1,\dots,p-1\}
. From the definition of
\mathcal{N}_{k}
, one has
(m,n)\in\mathcal{N}_{k}
if and only if

n_{i}=m_{i}\mod 2
for every
0\leq i\leq k-1
. For any such
i
, there are
\left(\frac{p-1}{2}\right)^{2}
choices for the pair
(n_{i},m_{i})
when both
n_{i}
and
m_{i}
are odd and

\left(\frac{p+1}{2}\right)^{2}
choices for the pair
(n_{i},m_{i})
, when both
m_{i}
and
n_{i}
are even. Hence, for every pair
(n_{i},m_{i})
, there are
\left(\frac{p-1}{2}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{p+1}{2}\right)^{2}=\frac{p^{2}+1}{2}
choices for pair
(n_{i},m_{i})
. Therefore,
\#\mathcal{N}_{k}=\left(\frac{p^{2}+1}{2}\right)^{k}
.
∎

Define a mass distribution
\mu
on
[0,1]^{2}
, such that for every
m,n\in\{0,1,\dots,p^{k}-1\}
, we have

\mu\left(\left[\frac{m}{p^{k}},\frac{m+1}{p^{k}}\right)\times\left[\frac{n}{p^{k}},\frac{n+1}{p^{k}}\right)\right)=\begin{cases}\left(\frac{p^{2}+1}{2}\right)^{-k}&(m,n)\in\mathcal{N}_{k},\\
0&\text{else}.\end{cases}

Let
U\subseteq[0,1]^{2}
be a set satisfying
p^{-(k+1)}<|U|<p^{-k}
. Then,
U
can intersect at most four intervals comprising
\mathcal{I}_{k}
. Hence,

\mu(U)\leq 4\left(\frac{p^{2}+1}{2}\right)^{-k}=4\left(p^{-k}\right)^{\frac{\log\left(\frac{p^{2}+1}{2}\right)}{\log p}}\leq 4\left(p|U|\right)^{\frac{\log\left(\frac{p^{2}+1}{2}\right)}{\log p}}.

Thus, by Proposition
6.1
,

\mathrm{dim}_{H}(\mathcal{F}_{0,\infty})\geq\frac{\log_{p}\left(\frac{p^{2}+1}{2}\right)}{\log p}

and the first part of claim
2
is established.

6.2.
The Fractal
\mathcal{F}_{F,\infty}

Recall the alphabet
\mathcal{A}
from Section
5.2.1
. The fractal
\mathcal{F}_{F,\infty}
is obtained through the
[p,p]
-morphism

\Phi_{F,p}:(\mathcal{A}\setminus\{B\})_{2}^{\infty}\rightarrow(\mathcal{A}\setminus\{B\})_{2}^{\infty},

which is defined in the following manner:

(6.3)

\displaystyle\Phi_{F,p}(A)=\begin{matrix}A&F&A&\dots&A&F&A\\
F&A&F&\dots&F&A&F\\
A&F&A&\dots&A&F&A\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\dots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
A&F&A&\dots&A&F&A\\
F&A&F&\dots&F&A&F\\
A&F&A&\dots&A&F&A\end{matrix},

\displaystyle\forall X\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\mathcal{A}_{units},\,\penalty\

\displaystyle\Phi_{F,p}(X)=\Phi_{p}(X).

By the definition of a
[p,p]
-morphism,

(6.4)

\Phi_{F,p}^{k}(A)=\begin{matrix}\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(A)&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(F)&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(A)&\dots&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(A)&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(F)&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(A)\\
\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(F)&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(A)&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(F)&\dots&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(F)&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(A)&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(F)\\
\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(A)&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(F)&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(A)&\dots&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(A)&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(F)&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(A)\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(A)&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(F)&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(A)&\dots&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(A)&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(F)&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(A)\\
\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(F)&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(A)&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(F)&\dots&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(F)&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(A)&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(F)\\
\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(A)&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(F)&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(A)&\dots&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(A)&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(F)&\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(A)\end{matrix}.

Moreover, by a simple induction one can see that for every
0\leq m,n\leq p^{k}-1
,

\displaystyle\Phi_{F,p}^{k}(F)=\Phi^{k}_{p}(F)[m,n]=\begin{cases}C_{NW}&\text{ if }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ m=n=0,\\
C_{NE}&\text{ if }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ m=0\penalty\ \text{ and }\penalty\ n=p^{k}-1,\\
C_{SW}&\text{ if }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ m=p^{k}-1\penalty\ \text{ and }\penalty\ n=0,\\
C_{SE}&\text{ if }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ m=n=p^{k}-1,\\
E_{N}&\text{ if }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ m=0\penalty\ \text{ and }\penalty\ 0\neq n\neq p^{k}-1,\\
E_{E}&\text{ if }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ 0\neq m\neq p^{k}-1\penalty\ \text{ and }\penalty\ n=p^{k}-1,\\
E_{S}&\text{ if }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ m=p^{k}-1\penalty\ \text{ and }\penalty\ 0\neq n\neq p^{k}-1,\\
E_{W}&\text{ if }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ 0\neq m\neq p^{k}-1\penalty\ \text{ and }\penalty\ n=0,\\
0&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

Define

\mathcal{I}_{k}:=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq m,n<p^{k}\\
\Phi_{F,p}^{k}(m,n)\neq 0\end{subarray}}\left[\frac{m}{p^{k}},\frac{m+1}{p^{k}}\right)\times\left[\frac{n}{p^{k}},\frac{n+1}{p^{k}}\right),

and let
\mathcal{F}_{F,\infty}:=\bigcap_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{I}_{k}
. Now
[
Fal90
, Proposition 4.1]
is used to bound the Hausdorff dimension of
\mathcal{F}_{F,\infty}
from above.

Proposition 6.5
.

[
Fal90
, Proposition 4.1]

Suppose a set
X
can be covered by
n_{k}
sets of diameter at most
\delta_{k}
, where
\delta_{k}\rightarrow 0
as
k\rightarrow\infty
. Then,

\mathrm{dim}_{H}X\leq\liminf_{k\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log n_{k}}{-\log\delta_{k}}.

To apply Proposition
6.5
, the number of non-zero entries in
\Phi_{F,p}^{k}(A)
is counted. Note that for
k\geq 1
,
\Phi_{F,p}^{k}(F)
contains
4(p^{k}-1)
non-zero entries, and
\Phi_{F,p}^{k}(A)
contains
2\frac{p-1}{2}\cdot\frac{p+1}{2}=\frac{p^{2}-1}{2}
copies of
\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(F)
and
\left(\frac{p+1}{2}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{p-1}{2}\right)^{2}=\frac{p^{2}+1}{2}
copies of
\Phi_{F,p}^{k-1}(A)
. Let
a_{k}
denote the number of non-zero entries in
\Phi_{F,p}^{k}(A)
. Then,

\displaystyle a_{k+1}=\frac{p^{2}+1}{2}a_{k}+2(p^{2}-1)(p^{k}-1),\,\,\,

\displaystyle a_{1}=p^{2}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle a_{k}=

\displaystyle p^{2}\left(\frac{p^{2}+1}{2}\right)^{k-1}+2(p^{2}-1)\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\left(\frac{p^{2}+1}{2}\right)^{i-1}(p^{k-i}-1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{p^{2}+1}{2}\right)^{k}\left(\frac{2p^{2}}{p^{2}+1}+\frac{2}{p-1}-\frac{4(p+1)}{p-1}\left(\frac{2p}{p^{2}+1}\right)^{k}+4\left(\frac{2}{p^{2}+1}\right)^{k}\right).

Then,
\mathcal{I}_{k}
can be covered with
a_{k}
boxes of side length
p^{-k}
, and hence,
\mathcal{F}_{F,\infty}
can also be covered by
a_{k}
boxes of side length
p^{-k}
. Therefore, by Proposition
6.5
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{dim}_{H}(\mathcal{F}_{F,\infty})\leq&\liminf_{k\rightarrow\infty}\frac{k\log\left(\frac{p^{2}+1}{2}\right)+\log\left(\frac{2p^{2}}{p^{2}+1}+\frac{2}{p-1}-\frac{4(p+1)}{p-1}\left(\frac{2p}{p^{2}+1}\right)^{k}+4\left(\frac{2}{p^{2}+1}\right)^{k}\right)}{k\log p}\\
&=\frac{\log\left(\frac{p^{2}+1}{2}\right)}{\log p},\end{split}

proving the second inequality in claim
2
.

6.3.
Proof of the Inclusion

Claim
1
is now established. Note that to obtain
\Phi_{0,p}
from
\Phi_{p}
, one identifies
A
and
B
and then, replaces all other letters with
0
. Therefore,
\mathcal{F}_{0,\infty}\subseteq\mathcal{F}(\textbf{C}^{(p)},\mathbb{F}_{p})
, and hence,

\mathrm{dim}_{H}\left(\mathcal{F}(\textbf{C}^{(p)},\mathbb{F}_{p})\right)\geq\mathrm{dim}_{H}(\mathcal{F}_{0,\infty})\geq\frac{\log\left(\frac{p^{2}+1}{2}\right)}{\log p}.

Similarly, since
\Phi_{F,p}(A)
is obtained from
\Phi_{p}(A)
by replacing
0
with
F
and
B
with
A
, then,
\mathcal{F}(\textbf{C}^{(p)},\mathbb{F}_{p})\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{F,\infty}
. Therefore,

\frac{\log\left(\frac{p^{2}+1}{2}\right)}{\log p}\leq\mathrm{dim}_{H}\left(\mathcal{F}(C^{(p)},\mathbb{F}_{p})\right)\leq\mathrm{dim}_{H}(\mathcal{F}_{F,\infty})\leq\frac{\log\left(\frac{p^{2}+1}{2}\right)}{\log p}.

Thus, Theorem
1.9
follows.

7.
Lemmata on Number Walls of Generic Sequences

With the proof of Theorem
1.9
now complete, all that remains is to prove Theorem
5.2
. The first step towards this is to establish a series of lemmas around the behaviour of number walls generated by non-specific sequences. Section
8
then refines these lemmas to the number wall of the
p
-Cantor and
p
-Singer sequence.

Before the aforementioned lemmata are stated, the following natural generalisation of a number wall is defined.

7.1.
Geometric Transforms of Number Walls

Let
S
be a doubly infinite sequence over a field
6
6

6

See Remark
7.3
as to why one uses a field
\mathbb{K}
instead of an integral domain
\mathcal{R}
.

\mathbb{K}
. By Definition
1.5
,
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\textbf{S})[m,n]=0
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
m<-1
. In the language of number walls, one can consider these rows to comprise an
infinite window
in
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\textbf{S})
, denoted
\mathcal{W}_{0}
. From this perspective,
(W_{\mathbb{K}}(\textbf{S})[-1,n])_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is southern inner frame of
\mathcal{W}_{0}
. Also by Definition
1.5
,
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\textbf{S})[-1,n]=1
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. But this is merely a convention -
(W_{\mathbb{K}}(\textbf{S})[-1,n])_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
need only be a geometric sequence to comply with Theorem
4.2
. This motivates the following generalisation of the number wall.

Definition 7.1
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a field, let
r\in\mathbb{K}\backslash\{0\}
,
a\in\mathbb{K}
and let
\mathbf{S}=(s_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a sequence over
\mathbb{K}
. Additionally, recall the notation from Figure
2
. Then, the
(r,a)
-Number Wall of
\mathbf{S}
over
\mathbb{K}
, denoted
W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r,a)}(\textbf{S})=(W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r,a)}(\textbf{S})[m,n])_{n,m\in\mathbb{Z}}
, is defined as the two dimensional sequence generated by applying the Frame Constraints (equations (
4.1
)) to the initial conditions

W_{m,n}=W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r,a)}(\textbf{S})[m,n]=\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} &0&&\textup{ if }m<-1\\
&a\cdot r^{n}&&\textup{ if }m=-1;&\\
&s_{n}&&\textup{ if }m=0;&\\
\end{aligned}\end{cases}

In other words, Definition
7.1
states that
W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r,a)}(\textbf{S})
is calculated identically to
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\textbf{S})
, but with
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\textbf{S})[-1,n]=a\cdot r^{n}
instead of
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\textbf{S})[-1,n]=1
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Hence, it is immediate that for any sequence
S
,
W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(1,1)}(\textbf{S})=W_{\mathbb{K}}(\textbf{S})
.

Remark 7.2
.

Whilst equations (
4.1
) do guarantee that zeroes in an
(r,a)
-number wall appear in square windows, it is not immediate that
FC3
remains well defined in this setup. That is, there is no reason
a priori
that the inner frame of any window in an
(r,a)
-number wall is comprised of geometric sequences, which is necessary to define
P,Q,R
and
S
in
FC3
. This is addressed in Lemma
7.5
.

Remark 7.3
.

Note, that
(r,a)
-number walls are only defined over fields and not integral domains. Standard number walls are well defined over an integral domain
\mathcal{R}
, since each entry is equal to the determinant of a Toeplitz matrix (Definition
1.5
), which are calculated using only addition and multiplication and hence always elements of
\mathcal{R}
. In contrast,
(r,a)
-number walls have no such connection to Toeplitz matrices, and therefore,
\mathcal{R}
is replaced by a field
\mathbb{K}
to ensure the division in equation (
4.1
) is well defined.

7.1.1.
Geometric Transform of a Sequence

The following transformation of a sequence is also crucial to this section.

Definition 7.4
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a field, let
r,a\in\mathbb{K}
and let
\mathbf{S}=(s_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a sequence over
\mathbb{K}
. Then, define the
(r,a)
-
geometric transform of

\mathbf{S}
is the sequence
\textbf{S}{(r,a)}=(a\cdot r^{i}\cdot s_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
.

7.1.2.
Relationship between Number Walls and
(r,a)
-Number Walls

For
r_{0},a_{0},r_{1},a_{1}\in\mathbb{K}
, and
\mathbf{S}
be a sequence over
\mathbb{K}
, the following lemma states how
W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r_{0},a_{0})}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)
relates to
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\textbf{S})
.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a field, let
r_{0},a_{0},r_{1},a_{1}\in\mathbb{K}
with
r_{0},a_{0}\neq 0
and let
\mathbf{S}
be a sequence over
\mathbb{K}
. Then, the two-dimensional sequence
W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r_{0},a_{0})}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)
is well defined in the sense of Remark
7.2
and it satisfies

(7.1)

W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r_{0},a_{0})}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)[m,n]=\frac{r_{1}^{n(m+1)}a_{1}^{m+1}}{r_{0}^{nm}a_{0}^{m}}W_{\mathbb{K}}(\textbf{S})[m,n].

Proof.

If either
a_{1}=0
or
r_{1}=0
, the conclusion follows immediately since
\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}=\{0\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
, and hence
W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r_{0},a_{0})}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)[m,n]=0
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{-1\}
. Therefore, assume that
a_{1},r_{1}\neq 0
.

First, it is confirmed that
P,Q,R
and
S
from
FC3
are well defined. Indeed, assume that

(W_{\mathbb{K}}(\textbf{S})[m+i,n])_{0\leq i\leq k\in\mathbb{N}}=x\cdot v^{i}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{ or }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ (W_{\mathbb{K}}(\textbf{S})[m,n+i])_{0\leq i\leq k\in\mathbb{N}}=y\cdot u^{i}

for some
x,y,v,u\in\mathbb{K}
. In other words, assume that the above parts of the number wall are geometric sequences. Then, assuming equation (
7.1
) holds,

\displaystyle\left(W^{(r_{0},a_{0})}_{\mathbb{K}}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)[m+i,n]\right)_{0\leq i\leq k\in\mathbb{N}}

\displaystyle=\frac{xr_{1}^{n(m+1)}a_{1}^{m+1}}{r_{0}^{nm}a_{0}^{m}}\cdot\left(\frac{r_{1}^{n}\cdot a_{1}}{r_{0}^{n}\cdot a_{0}}\cdot v\right)^{i}

or

\displaystyle\left(W^{(r_{0},a_{0})}_{\mathbb{K}}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)[m,n+i]\right)_{0\leq i\leq k\in\mathbb{N}}

\displaystyle=\frac{yr_{1}^{n(m+1)}a_{1}^{m+1}}{r_{0}^{nm}a_{0}^{m}}\cdot\left(\frac{r_{1}^{m+1}}{r_{0}^{m}}\cdot u\right)^{i}.

That is, equation (
7.1
) preserves geometric sequences and hence
P,Q,R
and
S
are well defined in
FC3
.

Equation (
7.1
) is now established by induction on
m
. The base case is given by
m=-1
and
m=0
, and it follows immediately from Definitions
7.1
and
7.4
. Therefore, let
M
be a natural number, and assume that for rows
m<M
formula (
7.1
) holds.

Equation (7.1) is now verified for an entry on row
M
in each of the three of the possible non-trivial cases. The strategy is the same for each: first apply one of the Frame Constraints from Definition
7.1
, then use the induction hypothesis, and finally show that it satisfies Lemma
7.5
.

Case 1:

W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r_{0},a_{0})}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)[{M-2,n}]\neq 0
.

In this case,
FC1
is applied, giving
W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r_{0},a_{0})}[M,n]\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)=

(7.2)

\frac{\left(W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r_{0},a_{0})}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)[{M-1,n}]\right)^{2}-W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r_{0},a_{0})}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)[{M-1,n-1}]\cdot W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r_{0},a_{0})}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)[{M-1,n+1}]}{W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r_{0},a_{0})}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)[{M-2,n}]}.

By the inductive hypothesis,

\displaystyle W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r_{0},a_{0})}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)[M-1,n]=\frac{r_{1}^{nM}a_{1}^{M}}{r_{0}^{n(M-1)}a_{0}^{M-1}}W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[M-1,n],

\displaystyle W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r_{0},a_{0})}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)[M-2,n]=\frac{r_{1}^{n(M-1)}a_{1}^{M-1}}{r_{0}^{n(M-2)}a_{0}^{M-2}}W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[M-2,n],

\displaystyle W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r_{0},a_{0})}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)[{M-1,n-1}]=\frac{r_{1}^{(n-1)M}a_{1}^{M}}{r_{0}^{(n-1)(M-1)}a_{0}^{M-1}}W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[{M-1,n-1}],

and

\displaystyle W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r_{0},a_{0})}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)[M-1,n+1]=\frac{r_{1}^{(n+1)M}a_{1}^{M}}{r_{0}^{(n+1)(M-1)}a_{0}^{M-1}}W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[{M-1,n+1}].

Therefore,

\displaystyle(\ref{rs1})

\displaystyle=\frac{r_{1}^{n(M+1)}a_{1}^{M+1}}{r_{0}^{nM}a_{0}^{M}}\cdot\frac{\left(W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[{M-1,n}]\right)^{2}-W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[{M-1,n-1}]\cdot W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[{M-1,n+1}]}{W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[{M-2,n}]}

\displaystyle\begin{subarray}{c}\hyperlink{FC1}{FC1}\\
=\end{subarray}\penalty\ \frac{r_{1}^{n(M+1)}a_{1}^{M+1}}{r_{0}^{nM}a_{0}^{M}}W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[{n,M}],

which finishes the proof in this case.

Case 2:

W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r_{0},a_{0})}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)[{M-2,n}]=0=W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r_{0},a_{0})}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)[{M-1,n}]
. This case is a simpler application of the method seen in Case 3 and hence, for the sake of avoiding repetition, the proof is omitted. The reader can verify equation (
7.1
) in this case by following along with Case 3, making only 2 minor changes:

•

Every instance of
M
replaced with
M+1
;

•

FC2
is used in place of
FC3
.

Case 3:

W^{(r_{0},a_{0})}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)[{M-2,n}]=0\neq W^{(r_{0},a_{0})}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)[{M-1,n}]

In this case, one applies
FC3
.
Given a window in
W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r_{0},a_{0})}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)
of size
l\in\mathbb{N}
, let
A^{\times}_{k}
be the entry of the inner frame that corresponds to
A_{k}
(recall Figure
2
). Define similarly for other values of the inner and outer frame.

Recall Figure
2
. Using notation from
F3
, the goal is to calculate
W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r_{0},a_{0})}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)[{M,n}]=H_{k}^{\times}
. To do so, one requires the values of
k,A^{\times}_{k},B^{\times}_{k},C^{\times}_{k},D^{\times}_{k},E^{\times}_{k},F^{\times}_{k},G^{\times}_{k},

P^{\times},Q^{\times},R^{\times}
and
S^{\times}
, where the latter four values are the ratios for the geometric sequences
(A_{i}^{\times})_{0\leq i\leq l+1}
,
(B_{i}^{\times})_{0\leq i\leq l+1}
,
(C_{i}^{\times})_{0\leq i\leq l+1}
and
(D_{i}^{\times})_{0\leq i\leq l+1}
respectively. The following diagram illustrates which rows and columns of
W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r_{0},a_{0})}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)
these are located in.

Figure 7.
If
H_{k}
is on row
M
and column
n
, then this diagram shows the relative position of the other entries in the inner and outer frame.

Figure
7
shows that, for instance

D_{k}^{\times}=W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r_{0},a_{0})}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)[M-1,n].

One then applies the induction hypothesis and the fact that equation (
7.1
) preserves geometric sequences to calculate that

\displaystyle W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r_{0},a_{0})}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)[M-1,n]

\displaystyle=\frac{r_{1}^{nM}a_{1}^{M}}{r_{0}^{n(M-1)}a_{0}^{(M-1)}}W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[M-1,n]

\displaystyle=\frac{r_{1}^{nM}a_{1}^{M}}{r_{0}^{n(M-1)}a_{0}^{(M-1)}}D_{k}.

By the same method, one obtains that

\displaystyle A^{\times}_{k}=\frac{r_{1}^{(n-1-l+2k)(M-1-l)}a_{1}^{M-1-l}}{r_{0}^{(n-1-l+2k)(M-2-l)}a_{0}^{(M-2-l)}}A_{k}

\displaystyle P^{\times}=\frac{r_{1}^{M-1-l}}{r_{0}^{M-2-l}}P

\displaystyle E^{\times}_{k}=\frac{r_{1}^{(n-1-l+2k)(M-2-l)}a_{1}^{M-2-l}}{r_{0}^{(n-1-l+2k)(M-3-l)}a_{0}^{M-3-l}}E_{k}

\displaystyle B^{\times}_{k}=\frac{r_{1}^{(n-1-l+k)(M-1-l+k)}a_{1}^{(M-1-l+k)}}{r_{0}^{(n-1-l+k)(M-2-l+k)}a_{0}^{(M-2-l+k)}}B_{k}

\displaystyle Q^{\times}=\frac{r_{1}^{n-1-l+k}a_{1}}{r_{0}^{n-1-l+k}a_{0}}Q

\displaystyle F^{\times}_{k}=\frac{r_{1}^{(n-2-l+k)(M-1-l+k)}a_{1}^{(M-1-l+k)}}{r_{0}^{(n-2-l+k)(M-2-l+k)}a_{0}^{(M-2-l+k)}}F_{k}

\displaystyle C^{\times}_{k}=\frac{r_{1}^{(n+k)(M-k)}a_{1}^{M-k}}{r_{0}^{(n+k)(M-1-k)}a_{0}^{(M-1-k)}}C_{k}

\displaystyle R^{\times}=\frac{r_{0}^{n+k}a_{0}}{r_{1}^{n+k}a_{1}}R

\displaystyle G^{\times}_{k}=\frac{r_{1}^{(n+k+1)(M-k)}a_{1}^{(M-k)}}{r_{0}^{(n+k+1)(M-1-k)}a_{0}^{(M-1-k)}}G_{k}

\displaystyle D^{\times}_{k}=\frac{r_{1}^{nM}a_{1}^{M}}{r_{0}^{n(M-1)}a_{0}^{(M-1)}}D_{k}

\displaystyle S^{\times}=\frac{r_{0}^{M-1}}{r_{1}^{M}}\mathbf{S}

Finally, one calculates
H_{k}^{\times}
using
FC3
:

H_{k}^{\times}=\frac{\frac{Q^{\times}E_{k}^{\times}}{A_{k}^{\times}}+(-1)^{k}\frac{P^{\times}F_{k}^{\times}}{B^{\times}_{k}}-(-1)^{k}\frac{S^{\times}G^{\times}_{k}}{C^{\times}_{k}}}{R^{\times}/D^{\times}_{k}}\cdotp

Substituting the above values gives

\displaystyle\frac{Q^{\times}E_{k}^{\times}}{A_{k}^{\times}}=\frac{r_{0}^{k}}{r_{1}^{k}}\cdot\frac{QE_{k}}{A_{k}}

\displaystyle\frac{P^{\times}F_{k}^{\times}}{B_{k}^{\times}}=\frac{r_{0}^{k}}{r_{1}^{k}}\cdot\frac{PF_{k}}{B_{k}}

\displaystyle\frac{S^{\times}G_{k}^{\times}}{C_{k}^{\times}}=\frac{r_{0}^{k}}{r_{1}^{k}}\cdot\frac{SG_{k}}{C_{k}}

\displaystyle\frac{R^{\times}}{D_{k}^{\times}}=\frac{r_{0}^{nM+k}a_{0}^{M}}{r_{1}^{(n(M+1)+k)}a_{1}^{m+1}}\cdot\frac{R}{D_{k}}

and hence

\displaystyle H_{k}^{\times}

\displaystyle=\frac{r_{1}^{n(m+1)}a_{1}^{m+1}}{r_{0}^{nm}a_{0}^{m}}\cdot\frac{\frac{QE_{k}}{A_{k}}+(-1)^{k}\frac{PF_{k}}{B_{k}}-(-1)^{k}\frac{SG_{k}}{C_{k}}}{R/D_{k}}

\displaystyle\begin{subarray}{c}\hyperlink{FC3}{FC3}\\
=\end{subarray}\penalty\ \penalty\ \frac{r_{1}^{n(m+1)}a_{1}^{m+1}}{r_{0}^{nm}a_{0}^{m}}\cdot H_{k}

as required.

∎

Lemma
7.5
implies the following corollary that relates the profile of
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\textbf{S})
to the profile of
W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r_{0},a_{0})}(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})})
.

Corollary 7.6
.

For any nonzero choice of nonzero
r_{0},r_{1},a_{0}
and
a_{1}
in
\mathbb{K}
, one has that

\chi\left(W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r_{0},a_{0})}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)\right)=\chi(W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})).

7.2.
Reflecting Finite Number Walls

Just as Lemma
7.5
showed that number walls behave well under geometric transforms, this subsection shows the same but for a different transformation: reflection. There are two axes along which a finite number wall is reflected: the vertical and the horizontal. Here,
vertical
and
horizontal
refer to the line of reflection. The former is dealt with first.

7.2.1.
Finite Number Walls under Vertical Reflection

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and consider a finite sequence
\mathbf{S}=(s_{i})_{0\leq i\leq\ell}
over a field
\mathbb{K}
. The following lemma shows that
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})
behaves well when the sequence
\mathbf{S}
is ‘reflected’ vertically around its midpoint.

Lemma 7.7

(Vertical Reflections of Finite Number Walls)
.

Let
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})
be the finite number wall of the finite sequence
\mathbf{S}=(s_{i})_{0\leq i\leq\ell}
over a field
\mathbb{K}
. Define
\overset{\textup{\tiny$\bm{\leftrightarrow}$}}{\textbf{{S}}}=(\overset{\text{\tiny$\bm{\leftrightarrow}$}}{s}_{i})_{0\leq i\leq\ell}
as the ‘reflection’ of
\mathbf{S}
; that is,
\overset{\text{\tiny$\bm{\leftrightarrow}$}}{s}_{i}:=s_{\ell-i}
for all
0\leq i\leq\ell
. Then
W_{\mathbb{K}}\big(\overset{\text{\tiny$\bm{\leftrightarrow}$}}{\textbf{{S}}}\big)
is the vertical reflection of
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})
. More precisely, let
m
and
n
be natural numbers satisfying
0\leq m\leq\left\lfloor\frac{\ell}{2}\right\rfloor
and
m\leq n\leq\ell-m
. Then,

W_{\mathbb{K}}\big(\overset{\text{\tiny$\bm{\leftrightarrow}$}}{\textbf{{S}}}\big)[{m,\ell-n}]=W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[{m,n}].

Lemma
7.7
is illustrated by Figure
8
:

Figure 8.
Left: The number wall of a sequence
\mathbf{S}
over
\mathbb{F}_{5}
. The zero entries are coloured in red, and the nonzero entries are in shades of grey. Right: The number wall of
\overset{\textup{\tiny$\bm{\leftrightarrow}$}}{\textbf{{S}}}
, defined as in Lemma
7.7
.

Proof.

By Definition
1.5
,

W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m,n]=\det(T_{S}(n;m)).

Similarly,

W_{\mathbb{K}}(\overset{\text{\tiny$\bm{\leftrightarrow}$}}{\textbf{{S}}})[m,\ell-n]=\det(T_{\overset{\text{\tiny$\bm{\leftrightarrow}$}}{\textbf{{S}}}}(\ell-n;m)).

It is easy to verify that,
T_{\overset{\text{\tiny$\bm{\leftrightarrow}$}}{\textbf{{S}}}}(\ell-n;m)
is the transpose of
T_{S}(n;m)
. As the determinant of a matrix is invariant under transposition, the proof is complete.
∎

There are two immediate corollaries of Lemma
7.7
:

Corollary 7.8

(Symmetry of Finite Number Walls Generated by Symmetrical Sequences)
.

Let
\mathbf{S}=(s_{i})_{0\leq i\leq\ell}
be a finite sequence over a field
\mathbb{K}
satisfying
s_{i}=s_{\ell-i}
for every
0\leq i\leq\ell
. Additionally, define
\overset{\text{\tiny$\bm{\leftrightarrow}$}}{\textbf{{S}}}=(\overset{\text{\tiny$\bm{\leftrightarrow}$}}{s}_{i})_{0\leq i\leq\ell}
as in Lemma
7.7
. Then, for all
0\leq m\leq\left\lfloor\frac{\ell}{2}\right\rfloor
and
m\leq n\leq\ell-m
,

W_{\mathbb{K}}\big(\overset{\text{\tiny$\bm{\leftrightarrow}$}}{\textbf{{S}}}\big)[{m,n}]=W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[{m,n}].

The second corollary is obtained by combining Lemma
7.7
and Lemma
7.5
.

Corollary 7.9

(Reflections of Finite
(r,a)
-Number Walls)
.

Let
\mathbf{S}=(s_{i})_{0\leq i\leq\ell}
be a finite sequence over
\mathbb{K}
and let
r_{0},a_{0},r_{1},a_{1}\in\mathbb{K}
be such that
a_{0},r_{0}\neq 0
. Furthermore, define
\overset{\text{\tiny$\bm{\leftrightarrow}$}}{\textbf{{S}}}
as in Lemma
7.7
. Then, for all
0\leq m\leq\left\lfloor\frac{\ell}{2}\right\rfloor
and
m\leq n\leq\ell-m
,

W^{(r_{0},a_{0})}_{\mathbb{K}}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)[m,n]=W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r_{0}^{-1},a_{0}r^{\ell}_{0})}\left(\overset{\text{\tiny$\bm{\leftrightarrow}$}}{\textbf{{S}}}^{(r_{1}^{-1},r_{1}^{{}_{\ell}}a_{1})}\right)[m,\ell-n].

Proof.

By Lemma
7.5
, one has that

\displaystyle W^{(r_{0},a_{0})}_{\mathbb{K}}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)[m,n]

\displaystyle=\frac{r_{1}^{n(m+1)}a_{1}^{m+1}}{r_{0}^{nm}a_{0}^{m}}W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m,n]

(7.3)

\displaystyle=\frac{(r_{1}^{-1})^{(\ell-n)(m+1)}\cdot r_{1}^{\ell(m+1)}\cdot a_{1}^{m+1}}{(r_{0}^{-1})^{(\ell-n)m}\cdot r_{0}^{\ell m}\cdot a_{0}^{m}}W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m,n].

Lemma
7.7
implies that

(7.4)

\displaystyle(\ref{eqn: r-s-reflect_1})

\displaystyle=\frac{(r_{1}^{-1})^{(\ell-n)(m+1)}\cdot r_{1}^{\ell(m+1)}\cdot a_{1}^{m+1}}{(r_{0}^{-1})^{(\ell-n)m}\cdot r_{0}^{\ell m}\cdot a_{0}^{m}}W_{\mathbb{K}}(\overset{\text{\tiny$\bm{\leftrightarrow}$}}{\textbf{S}})[m,\ell-n]

and a final application of Lemma
7.5
gives

\displaystyle(\ref{eqn: r-s-reflect_2})

\displaystyle=W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r_{0}^{-1},r^{\ell}_{0}a_{0})}\left(\overset{\text{\tiny$\bm{\leftrightarrow}$}}{\textbf{S}}^{(r_{1}^{-1},r_{1}^{{}_{\ell}}a_{1})}\right)[m,\ell-n]

∎

Lemma
7.9
shows that
(r,a)
-number walls behave well under vertical reflections. This motivates the following definition.

Definition 7.10
.

Let
\mathbf{S}=(s_{i})_{0\leq i\leq l}
be a finite sequence over a field
\mathbb{K}
and let
r_{0},a_{0},r_{1},a_{1}\in\mathbb{K}
. Then, define

(7.5)

\mathcal{V}\left(W^{(r_{0},a_{0})}_{\mathbb{K}}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)\right):=W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(r_{0}^{-1},a_{0}r^{\ell}_{0})}\left(\overset{\text{\tiny$\bm{\leftrightarrow}$}}{\textbf{S}}^{(r_{1}^{-1},r_{1}^{{}_{\ell}}a_{1})}\right)

That is,
\mathcal{V}\left({W^{(r_{0},a_{0})}_{\mathbb{K}}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)}\right)
is the vertical reflection of
W^{(r_{0},a_{0})}_{\mathbb{K}}\left(\mathbf{S}{(r_{1},a_{1})}\right)
.

7.2.2.
Horizontal Reflections of Finite Number Walls

Recall, that a finite number wall is an infinite 2-dimensional array over
\mathbb{K}
with only finitely many entries defined (Definition
3
). This allows horizontal reflections of finite number walls to be well defined.

Definition 7.11
.

Let
S
be a sequence over a field
\mathbb{K}
and let
\mathbb{K}\times\mathbb{K}
be the space of 2-dimensional infinite arrays over
\mathbb{K}
. Then, define
\mathcal{H}:\mathbb{K}^{\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}}\to\mathbb{K}^{\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}}
as

\mathcal{H}((W_{\mathbb{K}}(\textbf{S})[m,n])_{m,n\in\mathbb{Z}}):=(W_{\mathbb{K}}(\textbf{S})[-m,n])_{m,n\in\mathbb{Z}}.

Remark 7.12
.

One rarely considers horizontal reflections of number walls on their own. Instead, they are often paired with a rotation, as defined in the next subsection.

7.3.
Rotating Finite Number Walls

The goal of this subsection is to show how infinite number walls can often be deconstructed into infinitely many finite numbers walls, each rotated by some multiple of
90^{\circ}
and potentially reflected horizontally or vertically. To this end, the following function is introduced that rotates an infinite array over
\mathbb{K}
(recall that a finite number wall is an infinite array, as in Figure
3
).

Definition 7.13
.

Let
(W_{\mathbb{K}}(\textbf{S})[m,n])_{m,n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathbb{K}^{\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}}
be the number wall of a sequence
S
over
\mathbb{K}
and let
\mathbb{K}^{\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}}
be the space of 2-dimensional arrays over
\mathbb{K}
. Then, define the function
\rho:\mathbb{K}^{\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}}\to\mathbb{K}^{\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}}
by

(\rho(W_{\mathbb{K}}(\textbf{S}))[m,n])_{m,n\in\mathbb{Z}}:=(W_{\mathbb{K}}(\textbf{S})[-n,m])_{m,n\in\mathbb{Z}}.

That is,
\rho
is the clockwise rotation of
90^{\circ}
of
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\textbf{S})[m,n]
around
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\textbf{S})[0,0]
.

The next part of this subsection shows that rotated finite number walls always appear on the edges of windows.

7.3.1.
Rotating the Frame Constraints

For an illustration of the following setup, see Figure
9
.

Let
\mathbf{S}
be a sequence over a field
\mathbb{K}
. For
m,l\in\mathbb{N}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
(W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m+i,n+j])_{0\leq i,j\leq l+3}
be an
(l+4)\times(l+4)
square of entries in
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})
comprised of the following parts:

•

An
l\times l
window
\mathcal{W}
given by
(W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m+i,n+j])_{2\leq i,j\leq l+1}=(0)_{1\leq i,j\leq l}
,

•

The north inner and outer frame of
\mathcal{W}
are given respectively by

\displaystyle\textbf{A}:=(A_{i})_{0\leq i\leq l+1}:=(W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m+1,n+i])_{1\leq i\leq l+2}

and

\displaystyle\textbf{E}:=(E_{i})_{0\leq i\leq l+1}:=(W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m,n+i])_{1\leq i\leq l+2}.

•

The west inner and outer frame of
\mathcal{W}
are given respectively by

\displaystyle\textbf{B}:=(B_{i})_{0\leq i\leq l+1}:=(W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m+i,n+1])_{1\leq i\leq l+2}

and

\displaystyle\textbf{F}:=(F_{i})_{0\leq i\leq l+1}:=(W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m+i,n])_{1\leq i\leq l+2}.

•

The east inner and outer frame of
\mathcal{W}
are given respectively by

\displaystyle\textbf{C}:=(C_{i})_{0\leq i\leq l+1}:=(W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m+l-i+3,n+l+2])_{1\leq i\leq l+2}

and

\displaystyle\textbf{G}:=(G_{i})_{0\leq i\leq l+1}:=(W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m+l-i+3,n+l+3])_{1\leq i\leq l+2}.

•

The south inner and outer frame of
\mathcal{W}
are given respectively by

\displaystyle\textbf{D}:=(D_{i})_{0\leq i\leq l+1}:=(W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m+l+2,n+l-i+3])_{1\leq i\leq l+2}

and

\displaystyle\textbf{H}:=(H_{i})_{0\leq i\leq l+1}:=(W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m+l+3,n+l-i+3])_{1\leq i\leq l+2}.

Furthermore, define the inner frame ratios by

\displaystyle P:=\frac{A_{1}}{A_{0}}

\displaystyle Q:=\frac{B_{1}}{B_{0}}

\displaystyle R:=\frac{C_{1}}{C_{0}}

\displaystyle S:=\frac{D_{1}}{D_{0}}\cdotp

7.3.2.
Triangular Portions Adjacent to the Outer Frame of

\mathcal{W}

Next, define four further portions of
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{P}_{south}

\displaystyle:=\left\{W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m+l+3+i,n+1+j]:{0\leq i\leq\left\lfloor\frac{l+1}{2}\right\rfloor,\penalty\ i\leq j\leq l+1-i}\right\}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{P}_{east}

\displaystyle:=\left\{W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m+1+i,n+l+3+j]:{0\leq j\leq\left\lfloor\frac{l+1}{2}\right\rfloor,\penalty\ j\leq i\leq l+1-j}\right\}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{P}_{north}

\displaystyle:=\left\{W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m-i,n+1+j]:{0\leq i\leq\left\lfloor\frac{l+1}{2}\right\rfloor,\penalty\ i\leq j\leq l+1-i}\right\}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{P}_{west}

\displaystyle:=\left\{W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m+1+i,n+1-j]:{0\leq j\leq\left\lfloor\frac{l+1}{2}\right\rfloor,\penalty\ j\leq i\leq l+1-j}\right\}

The definitions from this subsection thus far are illustrated in the following diagram:

Figure 9.
An illustration of the window
\mathcal{W}
(red square of zeroes), its inner and outer frame (green and blue, respectively) and the portions
\mathfrak{P}_{north}
,
\mathfrak{P}_{east}
,
\mathfrak{P}_{south}
and
\mathfrak{P}_{west}
(black outlines).

Lemma 7.14
.

Consider the
(l+4)\times(l+4)
square portion of
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})
as defined above. Then,

\displaystyle\hypertarget{lem1}{1)}\penalty\ \penalty\ W_{\mathbb{K}}(\textbf{S})[m+l+3+i,n+l+2-j]={W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(S,D_{0})}(\textbf{H})}[i,j]

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{ for }\penalty\ \penalty\ {0\leq i\leq\left\lfloor\frac{l+1}{2}\right\rfloor,i\leq j\leq l+1-i};

\displaystyle\hypertarget{lem2}{2)}\penalty\ \penalty\ W_{\mathbb{K}}(\textbf{S})[m+l+2-i,n+l+3+j]=W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(R,C_{0})}(\textbf{G})[j,i]

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{ for }\penalty\ \penalty\ {0\leq j\leq\left\lfloor\frac{l+1}{2}\right\rfloor,j\leq i\leq l+1-j};

\displaystyle\hypertarget{lem3}{3)}\penalty\ \penalty\ W_{\mathbb{K}}(\textbf{S})[m-i,n+1+j]={W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(P,A_{0})}(\textbf{E})}[i,j]

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{ for }\penalty\ \penalty\ {0\leq i\leq\left\lfloor\frac{l+1}{2}\right\rfloor,i\leq j\leq l+1-i};

\displaystyle\hypertarget{lem4}{4)}\penalty\ \penalty\ W_{\mathbb{K}}(\textbf{S})[m+1+i,n-j]=W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(Q,B_{0})}(\textbf{F})[j,i]

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{ for }\penalty\ \penalty\ {0\leq j\leq\left\lfloor\frac{l+1}{2}\right\rfloor,j\leq i\leq l+1-j}.

In other words,

\displaystyle 1)\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathfrak{P}_{south}=\mathcal{V}\left(\left(W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(S,D_{0})}(\textbf{H})[i,j]\right)_{{0\leq i\leq\left\lfloor\frac{l+1}{2}\right\rfloor,i\leq j\leq l+1-i}}\right),

\displaystyle 2)\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathfrak{P}_{east}=\rho^{-1}\left(\left(W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(R,C_{0})}(\textbf{G})[i,j]\right)_{0\leq i\leq\left\lfloor\frac{l+1}{2}\right\rfloor,i\leq j\leq l+1-i}\right),

\displaystyle 3)\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathfrak{P}_{north}=\mathcal{H}\left(\left(W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(P,A_{0})}(\textbf{E})[i,j]\right)_{0\leq i\leq\left\lfloor\frac{l+1}{2}\right\rfloor,i\leq j\leq l+1-i}\right),

\displaystyle 4)\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathfrak{P}_{west}=\rho\left(\left(W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(Q,B_{0})}(\textbf{F})[i,j]\right)_{0\leq i\leq\left\lfloor\frac{l+1}{2}\right\rfloor,i\leq j\leq l+1-i}\right).

The first step towards proving Lemma
7.14
is establishing that the triangular portions
\mathfrak{P}_{south}
,
\mathfrak{P}_{east}
,
\mathfrak{P}_{north}
and
\mathfrak{P}_{west}
depend only on the inner and outer frame of
\mathcal{W}
that are adjacent to it. This is the goal of the following Lemma, which is illustrated immediately after it is stated. In this upcoming lemma, the
\pm
and
\mp
symbols are used to indicate that there are two separate cases.

Lemma 7.15
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a field and let
\mathbf{S}
be a doubly infinite sequence over
\mathbb{K}
. If
m,l\in\mathbb{N}
with
l\geq 5
are such that for every
0\leq i<l
,
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m,n+i]\neq 0
, then, the values of

(W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m\pm j,n+i])_{2\leq j\leq\left\lfloor\frac{l-1}{2}\right\rfloor,\penalty\ j-1\leq i<l-j+1}

are calculated solely using

(W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m\pm j,n+i])_{j\in\{0,1\},\penalty\ 0\leq i<l}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{ and }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ (W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m\mp 1,n+i])_{\penalty\ 1\leq i<l-1}.

Similarly, the same is also true when the whole setup is rotated 90 degrees around its centre. That is, if for every
0\leq i<l
,
(W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m+i,n])\neq 0
, then the entries of

(W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m+i+j,n\pm j])_{2\leq j\leq\left\lfloor\frac{l-1}{2}\right\rfloor,\penalty\ j-1\leq i<l-j+1}

are calculated using only
(W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m+i,n\pm j])_{j\in\{0,1\},\penalty\ 0\leq i<l}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{ and }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ (W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m+i,n\mp 1])_{0\leq i<l}
.

Lemma
7.15
is depicted below.

Figure 10.
Each dot represents an entry in
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})
, with the crossed blue dots being nonzero. Some dots have two different colours. The left image depicts the first part of Lemma
7.15
, and the right image the second part. Lemma
7.15
says that the grey (black, respectively) entries depend only on the dark (light, respectively) green and blue entries.

Proof.

The ‘plus’ case of the first part of Lemma
7.15
is established first. The proof proceeds by induction on
j
, starting with
j=2
. For the base case, recall
FC1
. By the assumption that
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m,n+i]\neq 0
for all
0\leq i<l
,
FC1
implies
W_{K}(\mathbf{S})[m+2,n+i]
depends only on
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m+k,n+i]
for
k\in\{0,1\}
and
0\leq i<l
, as required.

For the induction step, let
j\leq\left\lfloor\frac{l-1}{2}\right\rfloor
be a natural number and assume that the statement of Lemma
7.15
holds for
(W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m+j^{\prime},n+i])_{2\leq j^{\prime}<j,\penalty\ j^{\prime}-1\leq i<l-j^{\prime}+1}
. This is now split into two cases.

Case 1:

W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m+j-2,n+i]\neq 0
;

Case 2:

W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m+j-2,n+i]=0
.

Case 1:
The conclusion follows from
FC1
as in the base case.

Case 2:
There exists some window
\mathcal{W}
such that
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m+j-2,n+i]
is part of
\mathcal{W}
. Two further subcases are now stated:

Case 2.1:

W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m+j,n+i]=0
by the Square Window Theorem (Theorem
1.8
);

Case 2.2:

W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m+j,n+i]
is part of the inner or outer frame of
\mathcal{W}
.

Case 2.1:
Since
W_{\mathbb{K}}[m,n+i]\neq 0
for all
0\leq i<\ell
, the top row of
\mathcal{W}
has index less than
m
and hence the conclusion follows.

Case 2.2:
By
FC2
and
FC3
, the value of
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m+j,n+i]
depends only on the inner and outer frame of
\mathcal{W}
. Hence, if
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m+j,n+i]
depends on some entry in
(W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m-k,n])_{k\geq 2}
, either the inner or the outer frame of
\mathcal{W}
are part of
(W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m-k,n])_{k\geq 2}
. However, this contradicts the assumption that
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m,n+i]\neq 0
for all
0\leq i<l
.

To complete Case 2.2, one must also establish that the value of
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m+j,n+i]
does not depend on
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m-1,n+i]
for
i\in\{0,l-1\}
. This is shown by contradiction: indeed, if the calculation of
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m+j,n+i]
depends on
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m-1,n+i]
for
i\in\{0,l-1\}
, then this implies the existence of a non-square window. The proof is completed when
i=0
, and the case where
i=l-1
follows from symmetry of Lemma
7.7
. The following diagram aids in the proof:

Figure 11.
The left side of Figure
10
, with additional details that are described below.

Assume that, for some
0\leq i^{\prime}<l-2j
,
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m+j,n+j+i^{\prime}-1]
(the purple dot, above) depended on
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m-1,n]
(the yellow dot, above). This necessitates that
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m-1,n]
is in the north outer frame of some window,
\mathcal{W}
of size
l^{\prime}
(red dots, above), as the difference in the row index is too great to apply
FC1
(from the assumption that
j>2
), and since
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m-1,n]
being in any other part of the inner or outer frame of
\mathcal{W}
would imply that
W_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{S})[m,n+i^{\prime}]=0
for some
0\leq i^{\prime}<l^{\prime}
, contradicting the assumptions of the lemma.

As illustrated by the brown arrow in Figure
11
, The vertical length of
\mathcal{W}
is
j-2
. However, as illustrated by the pink arrows in the same image, the horizontal length of
\mathcal{W}
is
j+i^{\prime}
. Therefore, by the Square Window Theorem,
i^{\prime}=-2
, which is a contradiction.

The proof of the minus case, along with the second part of Lemma
7.15
is fundamentally identical to the first case. The only difference is that one applies rearranged versions of the frame constraints
FC1
,
FC2
and
FC3
.
∎

7.3.3.
Proof of Lemma
7.14

.

Lemma
7.14
Part
1
:
Lemma
7.15
shows that the calculation of
\mathfrak{P}_{south}
depends only on
H
,
D
and the zeroes on row
m+l+1
. In particular, it does not depend on the value of
m
nor any other parts of the number wall. Hence, one is able to change every other part of the number wall and, as long as
H
,
D
, and the zeroes on row
m+l+1
remain in place, the values of every entry in
P_{1}
remain unchanged. Therefore, by letting
m=-l-3
and
n=-1
, and by setting all the entries on row
m^{\prime}<m+l+3
to be zero
7
7

7

The values of
F_{l+1}
and
G_{0}
also need to be changed to
F_{l+1}=S\cdot D_{l+1}
and
G_{0}=S^{-1}\cdot D_{0}
to match the geometric sequence
(D_{i})_{0\leq i\leq l+1}
, the calculation of
\mathfrak{P}_{south}
becomes identical to the calculation of
\mathcal{V}{\left(W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(S,D_{0})}(\textbf{H})[i,j]\right)}
from the definition of
\mathcal{V}
(equation (
7.5
)).

Lemma
7.14
Part
2
:
As with
\mathfrak{P}_{south}
, Lemma
7.15
shows that
\mathfrak{P}_{east}
is fully determined given the eastern inner and outer frame of
\mathcal{W}
, along with the eastern most column of
\mathcal{W}
itself.

For ease of notation, define
\mathfrak{P}_{east}[i,j]:=W_{\mathbb{K}}(\textbf{S})[m+l+2-i,n+l+3+j]
.

The goal is to establish

(7.6)

\mathfrak{P}_{east}[i,j]=W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(R,C_{0})}(\textbf{G})[j,i]

for
{0\leq j\leq\left\lfloor\frac{l+1}{2}\right\rfloor,j\leq i\leq l+1-j}
. This is achieved by induction on
j
.

The base case is immediate: namely,

\mathfrak{P}_{east}[i,0]=W_{\mathbb{K}}^{(R,C_{0})}(\textbf{G})[0,i]=G_{i}

for all
0\leq i\leq l+1
. Hence, assume that equation (
7.6
) holds for all
j\leq u\in\mathbb{N}
and
j\leq i\leq l+1-j
.

Then, consider the entry
\mathfrak{P}_{east}[i,u+1]
for some
u+1\leq i\leq l-u
. This is calculated by rearranging either
FC1
,
FC2
or
FC3
. The plan is to show that the calculation of
